As far as regions are concerned, exports in Europe and North America have dropped by 24.5 per cent and 21.8% respectively, while exports in Asian countries have not been affected by a 6.1 per cent decline. From the second half of 2020, the volume of global trade has begun to gather in certain measurements. At this stage, two important political and economic developments involving Asia at one end and Europe at the other end are the ones that need to be carefully focused on and calculated for the outcome of our country's interests due to the outcome of the course of the next century. The first one -- the size of Asia -- was signed on November 15, 2020 in the 37th ASEAN Summit of Leaders in Vietnam, with a short name for RCEP. The agreement, signed by 15 countries in the Far East, Asia, and the Pacific Region after eight years of talks, is a broadly comprehensive free trade agreement. In addition, the same agreement is considered the world's largest free trade agreement, given the area of the countries who sided with it. It is also larger than the European Union and trade agreements between the United States, Canada, Mexico. Another development that is interesting is that the 15 countries who signed this agreement create such a comprehensive economic market, especially when they have many problems among themselves, including tensions in the South China Sea. The RCEP Agreement could be considered the most concrete indication that Asia will become the new economic power centre in the 21st century. This agreement calculates that exports across Asia-Pacific will increase by $668 billion in 2030. At the same time, the impact of exports in Europe is estimated to be a $9 billion reduction. Thus, the countries, particularly the European Union, are already working on the negative effects of this agreement. The political and economic development that concerns Europe is Britain's secession from the European Union, which means the decision taken as Brexit is in agreement. This will not only affect Europe, but will have significant consequences, as well as other parts of the world, as well as the outcomes and future developments. To make it even simpler, Asia has to make more effort to adapt to the new conditions, while Europe has to deal with a reverse acceleration as it takes an important initiative to influence the remaining parts of the new century. At such a time, the agreements made in all areas, especially trade and defence, which are expected to come to the agenda of the General Assembly today through the Foreign Commission, also matter to our country's perspective. There is no doubt that the importance of the agreement that we have reached with England as its common name is of great value to both parties in timing, intention and content. With the volume of $17 billion in trade in Europe, our relations with our largest trade partner, England, have historical depth. About 10 per cent of our total investments abroad are also present in this country. The agreement, which has been on our agenda with Britain, where we have extensive co-operation in each area, has been noted as being discussed since 2017. Another important issue is that Britain signed its first free trade agreement with our country following its agreement on the secession of the European Union. Our country has been prevented from losing $2.4 billion under a free trade agreement with Britain, which we have been trading under the Customs Union Agreement, which has been established so far with the European Union. The agreement also shows that customs duties have been lifted in industrial products, which make up 95 per cent of our exports. Our country's interests are also seen to be protected in the trade of ready clothes, white goods, automotive, food and agricultural products. Thus, it is clear that the free trade agreement signed with England has taken a proper step at the right time in terms of our bilateral relations, with the interests of our country being looked after in every way. Our hope is that the gaps will soon be resolved as a result of a comprehensive assessment for other sectors that have the potential to influence our relations with England. On the other hand, what policy will the UK follow with our country, especially in the issues of Cyprus and the Eastern Mediterranean, which want to separate from the European Union and set a new course for it? The new commercial and political partnership developed by England can also influence and yield to our interests the Customs Union Agreement, which we bring to our agenda with the European Union and demand a revision. Thus, it is seen that our country has seen a successful policy in time to assess the current developments between Britain and China and Far East Asia, where one end of the world's trade market is based. If the movement of trade goods between London and Beijing has begun to move through Ankara at this stage, each environment must calculate the center of gravity here correctly. In the 21st century, facing heavy agendas and rapid developments, Turkey must continue its course by maintaining its style and measure of Seljucian Turkish eagle, one head facing East to the West, holding East (with one paw) and the other holding West (with the other); that is the important thing. Asia is on our agenda, and Europe is on our agenda, and of course we are as much in Asia as we are in Europe, we will continue to exist, we must continue to increase our potential. We are Turkey, a state of great ambitions and vision, a state that prioritizes independence in every field, a ambitious, powerful, and radical state that desires peace for mankind in the world.
